, so that it does not commute with
\mathsf{E}
, which implies that repeatability and first-kindness will be ruled out. But can a measurement of
\mathsf{E}
not disturb some other observable? Note that
[\mathsf{E}(\pm),\sigma_{3}]=\mp\lambda\mathfrak{i}\sigma_{2}
. By item (i), a non-trivial observable
\mathsf{F}
will be non-disturbed only if it commutes with
\sigma_{1}

and
with
\sigma_{2}
, which is clearly impossible. Indeed, for qubits in the presence of conservation, non-disturbing measurements are permitted only if
[\mathsf{E},\mathsf{F}]=[\mathsf{E},{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]=[\mathsf{F},{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]=\mathds{O}
.

Now let us consider the case where an observable
\mathsf{E}
is measured by the Lüders instrument
{\mathcal{I}}^{L}
defined in Eq. (
1
). If
\mathsf{E}
is commutative, then it holds that
{\mathcal{F}}({{\mathcal{I}}^{L}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{*})=\mathsf{E}^{\prime}
is a von Neumann algebra (as the commutant of a self-adjoint subset of
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
), even in infinite dimensions
[
76
,
77
]
. But recall that the Lüders instrument is a first-kind measurement if it is compatible with a commutative observable. By item (ii) of Theorem
4.1
it follows that in the presence of a conservation law, a commutative
\mathsf{E}
admits a Lüders instrument only if
\mathsf{E}
commutes with the system part of the conserved quantity. Now let us consider an observable
\mathsf{E}
that may be non-commutative, but commutes with
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. Note that in this case, unless
\dim({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})<\infty
, then
{\mathcal{F}}({{\mathcal{I}}^{L}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{*})
is not necessarily an algebra, since in infinite dimensions there exist non-commutative observables
\mathsf{E}
such that
{\mathcal{F}}({{\mathcal{I}}^{L}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{*})\not\subset\mathsf{E}^{\prime}

[
78
,
79
]
. But since
\mathsf{E}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({{\mathcal{I}}^{L}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{*})
always holds, then
[\mathsf{E},{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]=\mathds{O}\implies N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}^{k}\in{\mathcal{F}}({{\mathcal{I}}^{L}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{*})
for all
k\in\mathds{N}
. That is, the Lüders measurement of
\mathsf{E}
commuting with
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
will fully conserve
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. Recall that in such a case, non-disturbance will not be ruled out for any observable
\mathsf{F}
that commutes with
\mathsf{E}
(see Corollary
H.2
). Indeed, it will hold that
\Delta{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}=\mathds{O}
, and so any
\mathsf{F}
will trivially commute with
\Delta{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. But does an observable
\mathsf{E}
commuting with
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
always admit a Lüders measurement in the presence of a conservation law? We shall now show that in the presence of a full conservation law, and where the apparatus part of the conserved quantity is highly non-degenerate, such measurements will require a large coherence in the apparatus preparation. This surprising observation can be seen as a “converse” WAY theorem.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}:=({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi,{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{Z})
be a measurement scheme for an instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. Assume that
{\mathcal{E}}
fully conserves an additive quantity
N={N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}+\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
, where
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
and
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
, and that
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
fully conserves
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. Define the subspace of
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
that is involved during the measurement process as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}(\text{meas}):=\bigcup_{\rho\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})}\supp(\Lambda(\rho))\cup\supp(\xi)\subseteq{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},

where
\Lambda
is the conjugate channel to
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
defined in Eq. (
5
). Then either
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}(\text{meas})
is contained within a single degenerate eigenspace of
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
, or
\mathrm{Var}\left({{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi}\right):=\mathrm{tr}[{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}^{2}}\xi]-\mathrm{tr}[{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\xi]^{2}
must be large. Additionally, if
{\mathcal{I}}
is an extremal instrument, and if
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}(\text{meas})
is not contained within a single degenerate eigenspace of
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
, then the quantum Fisher information
{\mathcal{Q}}({N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi)
as defined in Eq. (
13
) must be large.

Note that even if
{\mathcal{I}}
is not a Lüders instrument, by item (iii) of Lemma
C.1
it holds that if
{\mathcal{I}}
is compatible with observable
\mathsf{E}
then
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
fully conserves
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
only if
[\mathsf{E},{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]=\mathds{O}
. Moreover, let us note that the Lüders
\mathsf{E}
-instrument is extremal whenever the effects of
\mathsf{E}
are linearly independent
[
80
]
. The proof of the above proposition is provided in Appendix (
K
), and here we present a rough sketch. In the case that
{\mathcal{E}}
fully conserves
N
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
fully conserves
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
then both the expected value and the variance of
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
must not change as a result of the measurement interaction. That is,
\mathrm{tr}[{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\Lambda(\rho)]=\mathrm{tr}[{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\xi]
and
\mathrm{Var}\left({{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\Lambda(\rho)}\right)=\mathrm{Var}\left({{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi}\right)
for all
\rho\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
. It follows that if
\xi
is an eigenstate of
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
, i.e., if
\xi
has support only in a single degenerate eigenspace of
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
, then
\Lambda(\rho)
must live in the same eigenspace for all
\rho
. That is,
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
must be “effectively” fully degenerate, in the sense that
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}(\text{meas})
must be contained within a single degenerate eigenspace of
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
. This generalises an observation made in Ref.
[
81
]
, which held only in the case of Lüders measurements of sharp observables, implemented by normal measurement schemes satisfying the Yanase condition. But in many physically relevant situations
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
will not be (effectively) fully degenerate—for example, the apparatus may be a system with a conserved quantity
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
that is completely non-degenerate. In such cases, when the interaction between system and apparatus obeys a full conservation law, an instrument that fully conserves
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
can be implemented only if the apparatus preparation is not an eigenstate of
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
, which implies that
\mathrm{Var}\left({{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi}\right)
must be large. Finally, if the instrument is extremal, and
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
is not (effectively) fully degenerate, then for every pure state decomposition
\xi=\sum_{i}q_{i}P_{\phi_{i}}
, the uncertainty of
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
in
\phi_{i}
must be large, which implies that the apparatus preparation must have a large coherence as quantified by the quantum Fisher information.

4.2
Non-disturbance and operator spaces

Due to the Schauder–Tychonoff fixed point theorem
[
82
]
, all
\mathsf{E}
-channels
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
have at least one fixed state. However, it may be that none of these are faithful. In such a case, the fixed-point set of the dual channel
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
is not necessarily a von Neumann algebra, but rather forms an operator space
[
83
]
. This setting has been much less investigated, and in Appendix (
M.1
) we provide some novel analysis of the structure of such fixed-point sets. While the discussion thus far has been applicable for infinite-dimensional systems—except in some examples—in this section we shall always assume that
\dim({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})<\infty
.

We define the minimal support projection
P
on the fixed-point set
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
as

\displaystyle P=\min\{Q:Q\text{ is a projection, }\rho=Q\rho Q\,\forall\,\rho\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})\}.

(20)

In other words, for all projections
Q
and fixed states
\rho\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
such that
\rho=Q\rho Q
, it holds that
Q\geqslant P
. Note that
P=\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
if and only if
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
contains a faithful state, in which case
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
is an algebra, so that we recover the results of Theorem
4.1
. We now provide a generalisation of this result which accounts for situations where
P
may be smaller than the identity, i.e., where the
\mathsf{E}
-channel
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
may perturb all faithful states. Here, we define
P\mathsf{E}P:=\{P\mathsf{E}(x)P:x\in{\mathcal{X}}\}
and
P\mathsf{F}P:=\{P\mathsf{F}(y)P:y\in{\mathcal{Y}}\}
as restrictions of observables
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
in
P{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
\mathsf{E}:=\{\mathsf{E}(x):x\in{\mathcal{X}}\}
and
\mathsf{F}:=\{\mathsf{F}(y):y\in{\mathcal{Y}}\}
be observables acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}:=({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi,{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{Z})
be a measurement scheme for an
\mathsf{E}
-instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
, and assume that
{\mathcal{E}}
conserves an additive quantity
N={N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}+\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
on average, where
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
and
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
. If
P
is the minimal support projection on
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
, then the following hold:

(i)

\mathsf{F}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
only if
P\mathsf{F}P
commutes with
P\mathsf{E}P
, with
P\Delta{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P:=P{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}({N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})P-P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
, and with

\{[P\mathsf{E}(x)P,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]:x\in{\mathcal{X}}\}
.

(ii)

{\mathcal{I}}
is a measurement of the first kind only if
P\mathsf{E}P
is commutative and commutes with
P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
.

(iii)

{\mathcal{I}}
is repeatable only if
P\mathsf{E}P
is sharp and commutes with
P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
.

The proof is provided in Appendix (
M.2
) (Theorem
M.1
) and it follows from similar arguments as those used in Theorem
4.1
. That is, by noting that there exists a faithful fixed state in the subspace
P{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, we observe that the projection of the fixed-point set
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
onto the subspace
P{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
is a von Neumann algebra.

As a simple example, let us consider the case where
\mathsf{E}
is measured by a
nuclear
instrument. The operations of a nuclear instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
are written as
{\mathcal{I}}_{x}(\cdot)=\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{E}(x)\cdot]\sigma_{x}
, where
\{\sigma_{x}\}
is a family of states. It is simple to verify that in such a case,
P
is the minimal projection on
\cup_{x}\supp(\sigma_{x})
. Additionally, if
\mathsf{E}
is a norm-1 observable, and for every
x
the support of
\sigma_{x}
is contained within the eigenvalue-1 eigenspace of
\mathsf{E}(x)
, then such an instrument will be a repeatable measurement of
\mathsf{E}
. Every observable admits a nuclear instrument and, as shown in Corollary 1 of Ref.
[
4
]
, every instrument compatible with a rank-1 observable is nuclear. Now assume that
{\mathcal{I}}
does not disturb some observable
\mathsf{F}
. Since the dual
\mathsf{E}
-channel may be written as
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(\cdot)=\sum_{x}\mathrm{tr}[\cdot\sigma_{x}]\mathsf{E}(x)
, we observe that
\mathsf{F}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
implies that
\mathsf{F}(y)=\sum_{x}p(y|x)\mathsf{E}(x)
. That is, non-disturbance is possible only if
\mathsf{F}
is a classical post-processing of
\mathsf{E}
. But note that unless
\mathsf{E}
is commutative, this does not generally imply that
\mathsf{F}
must commute with
\mathsf{E}
. However, item (i) of Theorem
4.2
states that
P\mathsf{F}P
must commute with
P\mathsf{E}P
and with
P\Delta{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
. Given that
P\Delta{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P=P{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}({N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})P-P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P=\sum_{x}\mathrm{tr}[{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\sigma_{x}]P\mathsf{E}(x)P-P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
, then
[P\mathsf{F}P,P\mathsf{E}P]=\mathds{O}
implies that the commutation of
P\mathsf{F}P
with
P\Delta{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
simplifies to
[P\mathsf{F}P,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]=\mathds{O}
. Indeed, in the case where
\cup_{x}\supp(\sigma_{x})={\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
so that
\mathsf{P}=\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
, it follows that only observables
\mathsf{F}
that commute with both the measured observable
\mathsf{E}
, and with the system part of the conserved quantity
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, will be non-disturbed.

While the implications of the above theorem depend on the minimal support projection
P
on the fixed states of the measurement channel
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
, and hence on the specific measurement implementation, we may use the structure of the fixed-point set to obtain necessary conditions for first-kindness that depend only on the measured observable. In Appendix (
M.3
), we provide some necessary conditions for non-disturbance that are independent of conservation laws, showing that non-disturbance is intimately related to distinguishability. In particular, we show that if
{\mathcal{I}}
is a first-kind measurement of
\mathsf{E}
, then this observable must be a classical post-processing of a norm-1 observable
\mathsf{G}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
, and that there exists a family of states
\{\rho_{z}\}
that are perfectly distinguishable by a measurement of
\mathsf{G}
such that
\{{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\rho_{z})\}
remain perfectly distinguishable. Next, we use these results to obtain a quantitative form of the WAY theorem for first-kindness, presented below.

Theorem 4.3
.

Consider a measurement scheme
{\mathcal{M}}:=({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi,{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{Z})
for a nontrivial observable
\mathsf{E}
with the instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. Assume that
{\mathcal{I}}
is a measurement of the first kind, and that
{\mathcal{E}}
conserves an additive quantity
N=N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}+\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}
on average, where
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
and
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
.
For each outcome
x
associated with a non-trivial effect
\mathsf{E}(x)
, let
\mathcal{K}_{\max}(x)
and
\mathcal{K}_{\min}(x)
be subspaces of
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\max}(x):=\{\psi\in{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}:\ \mathsf{E}(x)\psi=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|\psi\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\min}(x):=\{\phi\in{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}:\ (\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x))\phi=\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|\phi\}.

\mathcal{K}_{\max}(x)
and
\mathcal{K}_{\min}(x)
are orthogonal, and for all unit vectors
\psi\in\mathcal{K}_{\max}(x)
and
\phi\in\mathcal{K}_{\min}(x)
, it holds that

\displaystyle|\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle|\leqslant\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|\left(\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|^{\frac{1}{2}}(1-\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(1-\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

(21)

For a proof, see Appendix (
M.4
) (Theorem
M.3
). We note that if
\mathsf{E}
commutes with
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, then Theorem
4.3
imposes no restrictions on first-kindness. This is because so long as
\mathsf{E}(x)
is non-trivial and commutes with
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, then the lower bound of Eq. (
21
) vanishes. On the other hand, if for any non-trivial
\mathsf{E}(x)
not commuting with
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
it holds that
\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle\neq 0
for some
\psi\in\mathcal{K}_{\max}(x)
and
\phi\in\mathcal{K}_{\min}(x)
, then
\mathsf{E}
admits a first-kind measurement only if either (i)
\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|<1
or (ii)
\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|<1
. This is so because if both (i) and (ii) are violated, i.e., if
\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|=\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|=1
, then the upper bound of Eq. (
21
) vanishes. It follows that, in such a case,
\mathsf{E}(x)
cannot have both
1
and
0
as an eigenvalue: such an observable must be highly unsharp. Indeed, we may also recover the first-kindness statement of Theorem
3.3
directly from Theorem
4.3
, which is presented as Corollary
M.3
in Appendix (
M.4
). This raises an interesting question: will Eq. (
21
) also hold if we abandon the requirement of first-kindness, and instead assume that the measurement satisfies the Yanase condition? This question is beyond the scope of the present paper, but the answer may highlight to what extent the necessary conditions for measurability and non-disturbance will continue to satisfy the “symmetry” witnessed so far in WAY-type theorems.

To demonstrate that Theorem
4.3
provides much stronger constraints than Theorem
3.3
, let us consider again the simple model of a binary observable
\mathsf{E}_{\lambda}:=\{\mathsf{E}_{\lambda}(+),\mathsf{E}_{\lambda}(-)\}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\simeq\mathds{C}^{3}
introduced surrounding Eq. (
19
). Recall that Theorem
3.3
did not rule out first-kind measurements of
\mathsf{E}_{\lambda}
for any
1/2<\lambda<1
. But now note that
\mathcal{K}_{\max}(\pm)=\spann\{|{\pm}\rangle\}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\min}(\pm)=\spann\{|{\mp}\rangle\}
,
\|\mathsf{E}_{\lambda}(\pm)\|=\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}_{\lambda}(\pm)\|=\lambda
,
\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|=1
, and
|\langle\pm|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}|\mp\rangle|=1
. By Theorem
4.3
, it follows that such an observable admits a first-kind measurement only if

\displaystyle 1\leqslant 2\sqrt{\lambda(1-\lambda)},

which cannot be satisfied for any
1/2<\lambda<1
; indeed, the above inequality is satisfied only if
\lambda=1/2
, in which case
\mathsf{E}_{\lambda}(\pm)=\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}/2
are trivial effects.

5
Conclusions

We have provided a number of general and operational bounds which capture measurement error and disturbance, with emphasis on the setting in which there is a conservation law—both “full” conservation, and the weaker notion of “average” conservation. We obtained new, quantitative versions of the WAY theorem, which generalise previous work in several respects, going beyond normal measurement schemes, and not assuming that the observable to be measured is sharp. The work presented surrounding the WAY theorem was also studied in the novel setting of sequential measurements for general pairs of observables, and the quantitative bounds were further refined by the analysis of the fixed point structure of the measurement channel in settings which have received scant attention.

We saw that the large apparatus coherence played a key role for measurability and non-disturbance in the presence of a full conservation law, pointing to the requirement of “large” apparatus. This points further to possible deep connections between the WAY theorem and the rapidly developing theory of quantum reference frames, analysed so far only when the conserved quantity has a conjugate phase
[
42
,
41
]
. While necessary, however, the large apparatus coherence was shown to not be sufficient for good measurements; we saw that conservation laws impose strict constraints on the error or disturbance for unsharp observables that admit definite values.

Our work suffers from the drawback that many physically arising conserved quantities are unbounded. Very recently, the measurability part of the WAY theorem for sharp target observables was proven in the setting of unbounded conserved quantities, where the conservation law is stated as the invariance of the unitary group generated by the conserved quantity under the action of the measurement interaction
[
84
]
. The measurability question for unsharp target observables, as well as the question of disturbance, should also be systematically studied when the conserved quantity is unbounded. This is a technically challenging endeavour and we save it for future work.

Acknowledgements.
M.H.M. acknowledges funding from the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme under the Marie Skłodowska-Curie grant agreement No. 801505, as well as from the Slovak Academy of Sciences under MoRePro project OPEQ (19MRP0027).
T.M. acknowledges financial support from JSPS KAKENHI Grant No.JP20K03732.

Appendix A
Properties of operations

Operations allow for the construction of an “operator-valued inner product”, which will be frequently used in this paper. For an operation
\Phi^{*}:{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})\to{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
, we define the sesquilinear mapping
\langle\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle\rangle:{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})\times{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})\to{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
by

\displaystyle\langle\langle A|B\rangle\rangle:=\Phi^{*}(A^{*}B)-\Phi^{*}(A^{*})\Phi^{*}(B),

(22)

to hold for all
A,B\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
. The following lemma shows that such a map mimics several important properties of an inner product.

Lemma A.1
.

For all
A,B,C\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
, the sesquilinear mapping defined in Eq. (
22
) satisfies: (i)
\langle\langle A|B+\lambda C\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle A|B\rangle\rangle+\lambda\langle\langle A|C\rangle\rangle
for all
\lambda\in\mathds{C}
; (ii)
\langle\langle A|B\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle B|A\rangle\rangle^{*}
; (iii)
\langle\langle A|A\rangle\rangle\geqslant\mathds{O}
; and (iv) the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\displaystyle\langle\langle A|B\rangle\rangle\langle\langle B|A\rangle\rangle\leqslant\|\langle\langle B|B\rangle\rangle\|\langle\langle A|A\rangle\rangle.

Proof.

(i) trivially follows from linearity of operations, while (ii) follows from the fact that an operation preserves the involution, i.e.,
\Phi^{*}(A)^{*}=\Phi^{*}(A^{*})
. (iii) follows from Kadison’s inequality, or the two-positivity of CP maps
[
85
,
86
]
. To show this, note that by Stinespring’s dilation theorem
[
87
]
we may write
\Phi^{*}(A)=V^{*}(A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})V
, where
V:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{K}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
is a linear operator. Since
\Phi^{*}
is sub-unital, it must hold that
\Phi^{*}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})=V^{*}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})V\equiv V^{*}V\leqslant\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{H}}}}
, with equality if
\Phi^{*}
is a channel, in which case
V
is an isometry. By the C* identity we therefore have
\|VV^{*}\|=\|V^{*}V\|\leqslant 1
, which implies that
\mathds{O}\leqslant VV^{*}\leqslant\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}}
. By Eq. (
22
) we may therefore write

\displaystyle\langle\langle A|B\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=V^{*}(A^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})\pi^{*}\pi(B\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})V,

(23)

where
\pi=\pi^{*}:=\sqrt{\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}}-VV^{*}}
. That
\langle\langle A|A\rangle\rangle\geqslant\mathds{O}
trivially follows.

Finally, we prove the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality which, for the case of channels, was proven by Janssens in Lemma 1 of Ref.
[
53
]
. The proof for the case of general operations is identical; by Eq. (
23
) we may write

\displaystyle\langle\langle A|B\rangle\rangle\langle\langle B|A\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=V^{*}(A^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})\pi^{*}\pi(B\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})VV^{*}(B^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})\pi^{*}\pi(A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})V

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\pi(B\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})VV^{*}(B^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})\pi^{*}\|V^{*}(A^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})\pi^{*}\pi(A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})V

\displaystyle=\|V^{*}(B^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})\pi^{*}\pi(B\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})V\|V^{*}(A^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})\pi^{*}\pi(A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}})V

\displaystyle=\|\langle\langle B|B\rangle\rangle\|\langle\langle A|A\rangle\rangle.

In the second line we have used the fact that for any self-adjoint operator
A\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}})
, it holds that
B^{*}AB\leqslant\|A\|B^{*}B
for all
B\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
, while in the third line we have used the C* identity
\|AA^{*}\|=\|A^{*}A\|
for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
∎

Note that the sesquilinear mapping in Eq. (
22
) does not satisfy the positive definiteness property in general, that is,
\langle\langle A|A\rangle\rangle=\mathds{O}
does not imply
A=\mathds{O}
. This plays an important role in the multiplicability theorem
[
86
]
, which can be seen as a consequence of Lemma
A.1
:

Corollary A.1
.

Let
\Phi^{*}:{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})\to{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
be an operation, and consider an operator
B\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
. The following hold:

(i)

If
\Phi^{*}(B^{*}B)=\Phi^{*}(B^{*})\Phi^{*}(B)
, then
\Phi^{*}(AB)=\Phi^{*}(A)\Phi^{*}(B)
for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
.

(ii)

If
\Phi^{*}(BB^{*})=\Phi^{*}(B)\Phi^{*}(B^{*})
, then
\Phi^{*}(BA)=\Phi^{*}(B)\Phi^{*}(A)
for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
.

Proof.

Let us first prove (i). If
\langle\langle B|B\rangle\rangle=\Phi^{*}(B^{*}B)-\Phi^{*}(B^{*})\Phi^{*}(B)=\mathds{O}
, then
\|\langle\langle B|B\rangle\rangle\|=0
. Therefore, by Lemma
A.1
we have for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
the following:

\displaystyle\mathds{O}\leqslant\langle\langle A^{*}|B\rangle\rangle\langle\langle A^{*}|B\rangle\rangle^{*}=\langle\langle A^{*}|B\rangle\rangle\langle\langle B|A^{*}\rangle\rangle\leqslant\mathds{O}.

This implies that
\langle\langle A^{*}|B\rangle\rangle=\Phi^{*}(AB)-\Phi^{*}(A)\Phi^{*}(B)=\mathds{O}
. Similarly for (ii),
\langle\langle B^{*}|B^{*}\rangle\rangle=\Phi^{*}(BB^{*})-\Phi^{*}(B)\Phi^{*}(B^{*})=\mathds{O}
implies that for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
we have

\displaystyle\mathds{O}\leqslant\langle\langle B^{*}|A\rangle\rangle^{*}\langle\langle B^{*}|A\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle A|B^{*}\rangle\rangle\langle\langle B^{*}|A\rangle\rangle\leqslant\mathds{O},

which implies that
\langle\langle B^{*}|A\rangle\rangle=\Phi^{*}(BA)-\Phi^{*}(B)\Phi^{*}(A)=\mathds{O}
.

∎

Lemma
A.1
also has the following useful consequence:

Corollary A.2
.

Let
\Phi^{*}:{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})\to{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
be an operation. Given the sesquilinear mapping defined in Eq. (
22
), for all
A,B\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
it holds that

\displaystyle\|[\Phi^{*}(A),\Phi^{*}(B)]-\Phi^{*}([A,B])\|\leqslant

\displaystyle\|\langle\langle A|A\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\langle\langle B^{*}|B^{*}\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\langle\langle A^{*}|A^{*}\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\langle\langle B|B\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(24)

Proof.

Let us first write

\displaystyle[\Phi^{*}(A),\Phi^{*}(B)]-\Phi^{*}([A,B])=\langle\langle B^{*}|A\rangle\rangle-\langle\langle A^{*}|B\rangle\rangle,

which gives

\displaystyle\|[\Phi^{*}(A),\Phi^{*}(B)]-\Phi^{*}([A,B])\|\leqslant\|\langle\langle B^{*}|A\rangle\rangle\|+\|\langle\langle A^{*}|B\rangle\rangle\|.

(25)

By Lemma
A.1
and the C* identity
\|A^{*}\|=\|A\|=\|A^{*}A\|^{\frac{1}{2}}=\|AA^{*}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}
for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
,
we therefore have

\displaystyle\|\langle\langle B^{*}|A\rangle\rangle\|

\displaystyle=\|\langle\langle B^{*}|A\rangle\rangle\langle\langle A|B^{*}\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\leqslant\|\langle\langle A|A\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\langle\langle B^{*}|B^{*}\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\|\langle\langle A^{*}|B\rangle\rangle\|

\displaystyle=\|\langle\langle B|A^{*}\rangle\rangle\langle\langle A^{*}|B\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\leqslant\|\langle\langle A^{*}|A^{*}\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\langle\langle B|B\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Inserting the above inequalities in Eq. (
25
) gives the bound in Eq. (
24
).
∎

Finally, we present the following useful properties of operations:

Lemma A.2
.

Let
\Phi^{*}:{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})\to{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
be an operation. For any effects
A\in{\mathcal{L}_{p}}({\mathcal{K}})
and
B\in{\mathcal{L}_{p}}({\mathcal{H}})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\Phi^{*}(A^{2})-\Phi^{*}(A)^{2}\|\leqslant 2\|\Phi^{*}(A)-B\|+\|B-B^{2}\|.

Proof.

This inequality (for channels) was given as Eq.(4) in Ref.
[
88
]
; the proof below follows Theorem 2 of Ref.
[
89
]
.
Let us first define
C:=\Phi^{*}(A)-B
for notational simplicity. Now, given that
\mathds{O}\leqslant A\leqslant\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}}
implies
A^{2}\leqslant A
, we may write

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(A^{2})-\Phi^{*}(A)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Phi^{*}(A)-\Phi^{*}(A)^{2}

\displaystyle=[C,B]+C\big(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{H}}}}-\Phi^{*}(A)-B\big)+B-B^{2},

and so we have the bound

\displaystyle\|\Phi^{*}(A^{2})-\Phi^{*}(A)^{2}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|[C,B]\|+\|C\big(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{H}}}}-\Phi^{*}(A)-B\big)\|+\|B-B^{2}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|[C,B]\|+\|C\|\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{H}}}}-\Phi^{*}(A)-B\|+\|B-B^{2}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|[C,B]\|+\|C\|+\|B-B^{2}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\|C\|+\|B-B^{2}\|.

In the third line we use the fact that
A
and
B
are effects which, given that
\Phi^{*}
is an operation, gives
\mathds{O}\leqslant\Phi^{*}(A)+B\leqslant 2\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{H}}}}
. This in turn implies that
\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{H}}}}-\Phi^{*}(A)-B\|\leqslant 1
. The inequality in the final line follows from Robertson’s uncertainty relation, by which we have

\displaystyle\|[C,B]\|=\sup_{\|\phi\|=1}|\langle\phi|\mathfrak{i}[C,B]\phi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\sqrt{\langle\phi|C^{2}\phi\rangle-\langle\phi|C\phi\rangle^{2}}\sqrt{\langle\phi|B^{2}\phi\rangle-\langle\phi|B\phi\rangle^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\|C\|\sqrt{\langle\phi|B^{2}\phi\rangle-\langle\phi|B\phi\rangle^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|C\|.

The final line follows from the fact that
\mathds{O}\leqslant B\leqslant\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{H}}}}
implies
\sqrt{\langle\phi|B^{2}\phi\rangle-\langle\phi|B\phi\rangle^{2}}\leqslant 1/2
.

∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
\Phi^{*}:{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})\to{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
be an operation. Assume that
\Phi^{*}(A)=\mathds{O}
for some
A\in{\mathcal{L}_{p}}({\mathcal{K}})
. It holds that

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(AB)=\Phi^{*}(BA)=\mathds{O}

for all
B\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
.

Proof.

First, let us note that for any
B\in{\mathcal{L}_{p}}({\mathcal{K}})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{O}\leqslant\Phi^{*}(ABA)\leqslant\|B\|\Phi^{*}(\sqrt{A}A\sqrt{A})\leqslant\|B\|\|A\|\Phi^{*}(A)=\mathds{O},

and so
\Phi^{*}(ABA)=\mathds{O}
. By the two-positivity of CP maps, it follows that for any
B\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
we have

\displaystyle\mathds{O}=\Phi^{*}(AB^{*}BA)\geqslant\Phi^{*}(AB^{*})\Phi^{*}(BA)\geqslant\mathds{O}.

The claim immediately follows.
∎

Appendix B
Faithful fixed states and von Neuman algebras

Recall that for channels
\Phi:{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})
, and their duals
\Phi^{*}:{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
, the fixed-point sets are defined as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi):=\{T\in{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}}):\Phi(T)=T\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*}):=\{A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}):\Phi^{*}(A)=A\}.

Lemma B.1

(Lindblad)
.

Assume that
{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi)
contains a faithful state. Then
{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*})
is a von Neumann algebra.

Proof.

Suppose
B\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*})
, and define the operator
\Phi^{*}(B^{*}B)-\Phi^{*}(B^{*})\Phi^{*}(B)=\Phi^{*}(B^{*}B)-B^{*}B
, which is positive due to the two-positivity of CP maps. Let
{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi)
contain a faithful state
\omega
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}[\omega(\Phi^{*}(B^{*}B)-B^{*}B)]=\mathrm{tr}[\omega(B^{*}B-B^{*}B)]=0.

But since
\omega
is faithful and
\Phi^{*}(B^{*}B)-B^{*}B
is positive,
\mathrm{tr}[\omega(\Phi^{*}(B^{*}B)-B^{*}B)]=0
implies that
\Phi^{*}(B^{*}B)=B^{*}B
. Corollary
A.1
therefore implies that for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
,

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(AB)=\Phi^{*}(A)B.

Therefore, if
A\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*})
, then

\Phi^{*}(AB)=AB
, and so
{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*})
is closed under multiplication and is therefore a
*
-algebra.
Finally, if
{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*})
is an algebra, then
{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*})=\{K_{i},K_{i}^{*}\}^{\prime}:=\{A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}):[K_{i},A]=[K_{i}^{*},A]=\mathds{O}\,\forall i\}
, with
\{K_{i}\}
any Kraus representation of
\Phi

[
90
]
, making
{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*})
a von Neumann algebra (as the commutant of a self-adjoint subset of
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
)
[
55
]
.
∎

If
{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*})
is a von Neumann algebra, it holds that for any self-adjoint operator
A\in{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*})
, the spectral measure of
A
is also contained in
{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*})
. In the case that
A
has a discrete spectrum, i.e.,
A=\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}P_{n}
, this implies that
\{P_{n}\}\subset{\mathcal{F}}(\Phi^{*})
.

Appendix C
Fixed points of instrument channels

Here we prove a useful result regarding the fixed-point structure of the
\mathsf{E}
-channel
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}
, describing a non-selective measurement of an observable
\mathsf{E}
, which we shall use in several places in this paper.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{I}}
be an instrument compatible with an observable
\mathsf{E}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. The following hold:

(i)

If
\mathsf{E}
is sharp, then
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})\subset{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}({\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}))\subset\mathsf{E}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

If
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
is a von Neumann algebra, then
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})\subset\mathsf{E}^{\prime}
.

(iii)

If
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
fully conserves a self-adjoint operator
A\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
, then
A\in\mathsf{E}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

All
\mathsf{E}
-compatible instruments
{\mathcal{I}}
admit a measurement scheme
{\mathcal{M}}:=({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi,{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{Z})
. Therefore, by the channel
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}
defined in Eq. (
3
), we may write
[\mathsf{E}(x),{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A)]=[\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)),\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})]
. Since
[\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x),A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}]=\mathds{O}
, and
\mathsf{Z}(x)
are positive operators, then by the sesquilinear mapping
\langle\langle A|B\rangle\rangle:=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A^{*}B)-\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A^{*})\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(B)
and Corollary
A.2
we obtain

\displaystyle\|[\mathsf{E}(x),{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A)]\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\langle\langle\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\|\langle\langle A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}|A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\langle\langle A^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}|A^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg).

(26)

Since
\mathsf{Z}(x)
are effects, it follows that

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)\rangle\rangle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)^{2})-\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x))^{2}\leqslant\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x))-\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x))^{2}=\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}.

On the other hand, we have
\langle\langle A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}|A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}\rangle\rangle={\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A^{*}A)-{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A^{*}){\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A)
and
\langle\langle A^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}|A^{*}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}\rangle\rangle={\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(AA^{*})-{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A){\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A^{*})
. We thus obtain from Eq. (
26
) the bound

\displaystyle\|[\mathsf{E}(x),{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A)]\|\leqslant\|\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\|{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A^{*}A)-{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A^{*}){\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A)\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(AA^{*})-{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A){\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(A^{*})\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg).

(27)

Now we may prove (i). If
\mathsf{E}
is sharp, then the upper bound of Eq. (
27
) vanishes and so for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
,
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(A)\in\mathsf{E}^{\prime}
. As such,
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}({\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}))\subset\mathsf{E}^{\prime}
. That
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})\subset{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}({\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}))
is trivial.

Now we prove (ii). Assume that
A\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
, which implies that
A^{*}\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
. But if
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
is a von Neumann algebra, this implies that
A^{*}A,AA^{*}\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
, and so the upper bound of Eq. (
27
) vanishes. Consequently, we see that for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
,
A\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})\implies A\in\mathsf{E}^{\prime}
, which implies that
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})\subset\mathsf{E}^{\prime}
.

Finally, let us prove (iii). Let
A
be a self-adjoint operator, and assume that
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
fully conserves
A
. By Definition
2
it holds that
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(A^{k})=A^{k}
for
k=1,2
, and so once again the upper bound of Eq. (
27
) vanishes, implying that
A\in\mathsf{E}^{\prime}
.

∎

Appendix D
Disturbance, commutation, and compatibility

The pair of observables
\mathsf{E}:=\{\mathsf{E}(x):x\in{\mathcal{X}}\}
and
\mathsf{F}:=\{\mathsf{F}(y):y\in{\mathcal{Y}}\}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
are
compatible
, or
jointly measurable
, if they admit a joint observable
\mathsf{G}:=\{\mathsf{G}(x,y):(x,y)\in{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}\}
so that

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathsf{G}(x,y)=\mathsf{E}(x),

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\mathsf{G}(x,y)=\mathsf{F}(y)\qquad\forall\,x\in{\mathcal{X}},y\in{\mathcal{Y}}.

(28)

If
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
do not admit a joint observable, then they are
incompatible

[
21
]
. Now let
{\mathcal{I}}
be an
\mathsf{E}
-compatible instrument, and assume that
\mathsf{F}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
. In such a case, we may choose
\mathsf{G}
as
\mathsf{G}(x,y)={\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{x}(\mathsf{F}(y))
, which satisfies Eq. (
28
). It follows that non-disturbance implies compatibility, and so for two incompatible observables
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
, no
\mathsf{E}
-instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
exists that satisfies
\mathsf{F}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
. Note that while non-disturbance requires compatibility, compatibility does not guarantee non-disturbance.
For instance, while any observable is compatible with itself, for every informationally complete observable the fixed-point set of its compatible channel is trivial. Indeed, the size of the fixed-point set of an
\mathsf{E}
-channel is strongly related to the amount of information given by
\mathsf{E}

as shown in Ref.
[
16
]
. Furthermore,
as shown in Ref.
[
4
]
, there exist pairs of compatible observables
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
where
\mathsf{E}
admits an instrument that does not disturb
\mathsf{F}
, but all possible
\mathsf{F}
-instruments necessarily disturb
\mathsf{E}
. This further demonstrates that unlike compatibility, non-disturbance is not symmetric.

As shown in Ref.
[
88
]
, the pair of observables
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
are compatible only if

\displaystyle\|[\mathsf{E}(x),\mathsf{F}(y)]\|\leqslant 2\|\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathsf{F}(y)-\mathsf{F}(y)^{2}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\qquad\forall\,x\in{\mathcal{X}},y\in{\mathcal{Y}}.

(29)

Commutation is a sufficient condition for compatibility; if
\mathsf{E}
commutes with
\mathsf{F}
, then there is a joint observable
\mathsf{G}
with effects
\mathsf{G}(x,y)=\mathsf{E}(x)\mathsf{F}(y)\equiv(\sqrt{\mathsf{E}(x)}\sqrt{\mathsf{F}(y)})^{*}(\sqrt{\mathsf{E}(x)}\sqrt{\mathsf{F}(y)})
. On the other hand, if either
\mathsf{E}
or
\mathsf{F}
is sharp, in which case the upper bound of Eq. (
29
) vanishes, then commutation is a necessary condition for compatibility
[
91
]
. For two non-commuting observables to be compatible, therefore, their effects must be sufficiently unsharp. We now provide a bound for the disturbance of
\mathsf{F}
by an
\mathsf{E}
-instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
, in terms of the commutation between the effects of
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
.

Proposition D.1
.

Consider the observables
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, and let
\|\delta(y)\|
be the disturbance of the effects of
\mathsf{F}
caused by an
\mathsf{E}
-instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
. Then for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
it holds that

\displaystyle\|[\mathsf{E}(x),\mathsf{F}(y)]\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\delta(y)\|+2\|\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y)^{2})-{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y))^{2}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(30)

If
\mathsf{F}
is non-disturbed by
{\mathcal{I}}
, that is, if
\delta=0
, then for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
it holds that

\displaystyle\|[\mathsf{E}(x),\mathsf{F}(y)]\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\|\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y)^{2})-\mathsf{F}(y)^{2}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(31)

Proof.

By Eq. (
8
), we may write

\displaystyle[\mathsf{E}(x),\mathsf{F}(y)]=[\delta(y),\mathsf{E}(x)]+[\mathsf{E}(x),{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y))].

(32)

Every
\mathsf{E}
-instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
admits a measurement scheme
{\mathcal{M}}:=({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi,{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{Z})
. Using the channel
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}
defined in Eq. (
3
), we may therefore write
[\mathsf{E}(x),{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y))]=[\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)),\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{F}(y)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})]
.
Given that
[\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x),\mathsf{F}(y)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}]=\mathds{O}
, then by the sesquilinear mapping
\langle\langle A|B\rangle\rangle:=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A^{*}B)-\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A^{*})\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(B)
and Corollary
A.2
, we obtain from Eq. (
32
) the bound

\displaystyle\|[\mathsf{E}(x),\mathsf{F}(y)]\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|[\delta(y),\mathsf{E}(x)]\|+2\|\langle\langle\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\langle\langle\mathsf{F}(y)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}|\mathsf{F}(y)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(33)

Since
\mathsf{E}(x)
is an effect, then as shown in Lemma
A.2
we have
\|[\delta(y),\mathsf{E}(x)]\|\leqslant\|\delta(y)\|
. As shown in Lemma
C.1
, we have
\langle\langle\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)\rangle\rangle\leqslant\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}
and
\langle\langle\mathsf{F}(y)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}|\mathsf{F}(y)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}\rangle\rangle={\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y)^{2})-{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y))^{2}
. We therefore obtain from Eq. (
33
) the bound given in Eq. (
30
). If
\mathsf{F}
is non-disturbed by
{\mathcal{I}}
, then
\|\delta(y)\|=0
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(\mathsf{F}(y))^{2}=\mathsf{F}(y)^{2}
for all
y
. We thus arrive at Eq. (
31
).
∎

We see that when
\mathsf{E}
commutes with
\mathsf{F}
the lower bound of Eq. (
31
) vanishes, in which case Proposition
D.1
does not prohibit non-disturbance. Indeed, in the case of commuting observables there always exists a non-disturbing instrument; since
\mathsf{E}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({{\mathcal{I}}^{L}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{*})
always holds, where
{\mathcal{I}}^{L}
is the Lüders
\mathsf{E}
-instrument defined in Eq. (
1
), then a Lüders measurement of
\mathsf{E}
is guaranteed not to disturb any
\mathsf{F}
commuting with
\mathsf{E}

[
63
]
. On the other hand, if
\mathsf{E}
does not commute with
\mathsf{F}
, then Proposition
D.1
allows us to obtain a lower bound for the disturbance that results given any
\mathsf{E}
-compatible instrument, determined only by the unsharpness and non-commutation of
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
:

Corollary D.1
.

Consider the setup of Proposition
D.1
. For all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
, it also holds that

\displaystyle\|[\mathsf{E}(x),\mathsf{F}(y)]\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\delta(y)\|+2\|\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(2\|\delta(y)\|+\|\mathsf{F}(y)-\mathsf{F}(y)^{2}\|\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(34)

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{F}(y)
are effects and
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}
is a channel, then by Lemma
A.2
we have
\|{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y)^{2})-{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y))^{2}\|\leqslant 2\|\delta(y)\|+\|\mathsf{F}(y)-\mathsf{F}(y)^{2}\|
. As such, Eq. (
34
) is obtained directly from Eq. (
30
).
∎

Note that while Corollary
D.1
provides a lower bound for the disturbance, which is strictly positive whenever either
\mathsf{E}
or
\mathsf{F}
is sharp and these observables do not commute, such a lower bound will differ depending on whether
\mathsf{E}
or
\mathsf{F}
is sharp; if
\mathsf{E}
is sharp, we have
\delta\geqslant\max_{x,y}\|[\mathsf{E}(x),\mathsf{F}(y)]\|
, whereas if
\mathsf{F}
is sharp but
\mathsf{E}
is unsharp, the lower bound for the disturbance may be smaller. Let us illustrate this with the following example. Consider a system
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\simeq\mathds{C}^{2}
, with the orthonormal basis
\{|0\rangle,|1\rangle\}
, and define
|\pm\rangle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|0\rangle\pm|1\rangle)
. Now consider a pair of binary observables
\mathsf{A}=\{\mathsf{A}(a):a=0,1\}
and
\mathsf{B}_{\lambda}=\{\mathsf{B}_{\lambda}(b):b=\pm\}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, defined by
\mathsf{A}(a)=|a\rangle\langle a|
and
\mathsf{B}_{\lambda}(b)=\lambda|b\rangle\langle b|+(1-\lambda)\frac{\mathds{1}}{2}
for some
0\leqslant\lambda\leqslant 1
. It is simple to verify that
\|[\mathsf{A}(a),\mathsf{B}_{\lambda}(b)]\|=\frac{\lambda}{2}
for any
a=0,1
and
b=\pm
. Now we may evaluate the disturbance of one of these observables caused by a Lüders measurement of the other. The disturbance of
\mathsf{B}_{\lambda}(b)
by a Lüders measurement of
\mathsf{A}
reads
\|\delta(b)\|=\frac{\lambda}{2}
for each
b
. Since
\mathsf{A}
is sharp, then by setting
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{F}=\mathsf{B}_{\lambda}
, we see that the inequality in Eq. (
34
) is tight. On the other hand, the disturbance of
\mathsf{A}(a)
by a Lüders measurement of
\mathsf{B}_{\lambda}
reads
\|\delta(a)\|=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-\lambda^{2}}}{2}
for each
a
, which is smaller than
\frac{\lambda}{2}
for
0<\lambda<1
.

Let us now consider the case of non-disturbance more carefully. First, let us note that when we set
\|\delta(y)\|=0
, Eq. (
34
) reduces to the compatibility bound of Eq. (
29
), and states that for non-disturbance to be possible when
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
do not commute, then both observables must be sufficiently unsharp so as to be compatible. To be sure, compatibility is a necessary condition for non-disturbance, and the fact that Eq. (
34
) does not contradict the compatibility bound is not surprising. On the other hand, in the case of non-disturbance this bound is also not very informative—it is possible for two observables to be compatible, while a measurement of one still disturbs the other. To gain a better understanding of non-disturbance, let us consider instead Eq. (
31
), the upper bound of which is smaller than the upper bound in Eq. (
34
) when we set
\|\delta(y)\|=0
, and vanishes if both
\mathsf{F}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
and
\mathsf{F}^{2}:=\{\mathsf{F}(y)^{2}:y\in{\mathcal{Y}}\}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
hold. We immediately see that while unsharpness of both
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
is necessary for non-disturbance when
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
do not commute, it is not sufficient; as shown in Ref.
[
4
]
there are at least two classes of unsharp observables
\mathsf{F}
where given
any
instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e., including instruments that measure an unsharp observable
\mathsf{E}
that does not commute with
\mathsf{F}
but is still compatible with
\mathsf{F}
, it holds that
\mathsf{F}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
guarantees
\mathsf{F}^{2}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
: if
\mathsf{F}
is a rank-1 observable, or if
\mathsf{F}
is an “informationally equivalent coarse-graining” of a sharp observable.

Let us consider the first option. If
\mathsf{F}
is a rank-1 observable, then all the effects of
\mathsf{F}
may be written as
\mathsf{F}(y)=\lambda_{y}P_{y}
, where
P_{y}
is a rank-1 projection operator and
\lambda_{y}\in(0,1]
. As shown in
[
92
]
, all observables
\mathsf{E}
that are compatible with a rank-1 observable
\mathsf{F}
are the post-processings of
\mathsf{F}
, that is, the effects of
\mathsf{E}
may be written as
\mathsf{E}(x)=\sum_{y}p(x|y)\mathsf{F}(y)
, where
\{p(x|y)\}
is a family of non-negative numbers satisfying
\sum_{x}p(x|y)=1
for all
y
. It follows that so long as
\mathsf{F}
is a non-commutative rank-1 observable, then there exists an unsharp observable
\mathsf{E}
that is compatible with
\mathsf{F}
but does not commute with
\mathsf{F}
. But note that
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y))=\mathsf{F}(y)
if and only if
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{y})=P_{y}
. As such,
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y)^{2})=\lambda_{y}^{2}{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{y})=\lambda_{y}^{2}P_{y}=\mathsf{F}(y)^{2}
. It follows that
\mathsf{F}
will be non-disturbed by an
\mathsf{E}
-compatible instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
only if
\mathsf{E}
commutes with
\mathsf{F}
.

Let us now consider the second option. We say that
\mathsf{F}
is an informationally equivalent coarse-graining of a sharp observable
\mathsf{G}:=\{\mathsf{G}(z):z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\}
if there exists an invertible
stochastic matrix
M
such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}(y)=\sum_{z}M_{y,z}\mathsf{G}(z),

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}(z)=\sum_{y}M_{z,y}^{-1}\mathsf{F}(y).

\mathsf{F}
and
\mathsf{G}
are informationally equivalent because a measurement of
\mathsf{F}
produces different probability distributions for two states
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
if and only if these states produce different probability distributions given a measurement of
\mathsf{G}
.
Since
\mathsf{G}
is sharp, then
\mathsf{F}(y)^{2}=\sum_{z}M_{y,z}^{2}\mathsf{G}(z)
. Now assume that
\mathsf{F}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
. It is simple to verify that this implies
\mathsf{G}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
. Therefore, we have
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{F}(y)^{2})=\sum_{z}M_{y,z}^{2}{\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathsf{G}(z))=\sum_{z}M_{y,z}^{2}\mathsf{G}(z)=\mathsf{F}(y)^{2}
. Once again,
\mathsf{F}
will be non-disturbed by an
\mathsf{E}
-compatible instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
only if
\mathsf{E}
commutes with
\mathsf{F}
.

Both of the above examples offer a very simple interpretation in terms of compatibility. If
\mathsf{F}
is a rank-1 observable, then non-disturbance of
\mathsf{F}
implies non-disturbance of sharp rank-1 effects
P_{y}
. Since non-disturbance requires compatibility, this implies that
\mathsf{E}
must commute with all
P_{y}
, and hence with
\mathsf{F}
.
On the other hand, if
\mathsf{F}
is a classical coarse-graining of a sharp observable
\mathsf{G}
, then non-disturbance of
\mathsf{F}
implies non-disturbance of
\mathsf{G}
, and by compatibility
\mathsf{E}
must commute with
\mathsf{G}
. Since the effects of
\mathsf{F}
are constructed as a mixture of the (projective) effects of
\mathsf{G}
, this concludes that
\mathsf{E}
must commute with
\mathsf{F}
.

Appendix E
Properties of repeatable instruments

In this section, we prove a series of useful results regarding the structure of repeatable instruments, and the measurement schemes that implement them.

Proposition E.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}:=({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi,{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{Z})
be a measurement scheme for an
\mathsf{E}
-compatible instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. If
{\mathcal{I}}
is repeatable, then the following hold:

(i)

For all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
n\in\mathds{N}
, it holds that
\mathsf{E}(x)=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)^{n}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)^{n})
.

(ii)

For all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, it holds that
\mathsf{E}(x)
and
\mathsf{Z}(x)
have
1
as an eigenvalue, and so there exist projection operators
\mathsf{P}(x)\in{\mathcal{L}_{p}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
and
\mathsf{Q}(x)\in{\mathcal{L}_{p}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
which project onto the eigenvalue-1 eigenspaces of
\mathsf{E}(x)
and
\mathsf{Z}(x)
, respectively.

(iii)

For all
x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}
, it holds that
\mathsf{P}(x)\mathsf{E}(y)=\mathsf{P}(x)\mathsf{P}(y)=\delta_{x,y}\mathsf{P}(x)
and
\mathsf{Q}(x)\mathsf{Z}(y)=\mathsf{Q}(x)\mathsf{Q}(y)=\delta_{x,y}\mathsf{Q}(x)
.

(iv)

For all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
, it holds that
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}A)=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})=\mathds{O}
and
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Q}^{c}(x)A)=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Q}^{c}(x))=\mathds{O}
, where
\mathsf{P}^{c}(x):=\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{P}(x)
and
\mathsf{Q}^{c}(x):=\mathsf{Z}(x)-\mathsf{Q}(x)
.

(v)

For all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
, it holds that
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A)=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{R}A\mathsf{R})
, where
\mathsf{R}:=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x)
.

(vi)

For all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
, and
B\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
, it holds that
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(\mathsf{E}(x)A)={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(A\mathsf{E}(x))={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x))
and
\Lambda^{*}(\mathsf{Z}(x)B)=\Lambda^{*}(B\mathsf{Z}(x))=\Lambda^{*}(\mathsf{Q}(x)B\mathsf{Q}(x))
.

(vii)

For all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
, it holds that
{\mathcal{I}}_{x}^{*}(A)={\mathcal{I}}_{x}^{*}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x))
.

Proof.

(i):

The repeatability condition implies that for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, it holds that
\mathsf{E}(x)={\mathcal{I}}_{x}^{*}(\mathsf{E}(x))=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x))
. It follows that for any state
\rho\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}[\rho\mathsf{E}(x)]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}[\rho\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x))]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{tr}[\rho\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\rho\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)^{2})]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{tr}[\rho\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\rho\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x))]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}[\rho\mathsf{E}(x)].

Here, the second line follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the
third line follows from the fact that
\mathsf{E}(x)
and
\mathsf{Z}(x)
are effects and so
\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}\leqslant\mathsf{E}(x)
and
\mathsf{Z}(x)^{2}\leqslant\mathsf{Z}(x)
, and the final line follows from the fact that repeatability implies first-kindness and that
{\mathcal{M}}
is a measurement scheme for
\mathsf{E}
. As the second inequality must be an equality, we thus have
\mathsf{E}(x)=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)^{n}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)^{n})
for
n=1,2
. To show that the relations hold for all
n\in\mathds{N}
, it suffices to show that for all
\rho
, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the above arguments implies

\displaystyle 0\leqslant\mathrm{tr}[\rho\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}((\mathsf{E}(x)^{n}-\mathsf{E}(x)^{n+1})\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{tr}[\rho\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)^{2(n-1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\rho\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}((\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{E}(x)^{2})^{2}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{tr}[\rho\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)^{2(n-1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\rho\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}((\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{E}(x)^{2})\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})]^{\frac{1}{2}}=0,

and so it holds that
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}((\mathsf{E}(x)^{n}-\mathsf{E}(x)^{n+1})\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})=\mathds{O}
. Similar steps show that
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes(\mathsf{Z}(x)^{n}-\mathsf{Z}(x)^{n+1}))=\mathds{O}
. The claims are thus obtained by induction.

(ii):

Note that for any operation
\Phi^{*}:{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})\to{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
, it holds that
\|\Phi^{*}(A)\|\leqslant\|A\|
for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
. As such, by (i) we have

\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|=\|\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})\|\leqslant\|\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}\|=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|^{2}
. But since
\mathsf{E}(x)
is an effect it also holds that
\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|\geqslant\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|^{2}
. It follows that
\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|
is either zero or one. As we assume that
\mathsf{E}(x)
is not vanishing, then
\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|=1
follows. Similarly, we have
1=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|=\|\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x))\|\leqslant\|\mathsf{Z}(x)\|
, and since
\mathsf{Z}(x)
is an effect, then it must hold that
\|\mathsf{Z}(x)\|=1
.

Now we shall show that
\mathsf{E}(x)
has
1
as an eigenvalue, i.e., there exists a unit-vector
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
such that
\mathsf{E}(x)\psi=\psi
. If this is not so, then we would have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{E}(x)^{n}=\mathds{O}
, which would contradict (i). Therefore, there exists a projection operator
\mathsf{P}(x)
that projects onto the eigenvalue-1 eigenspace of
\mathsf{E}(x)
. Similar arguments hold for
\mathsf{Z}(x)
and
\mathsf{Q}(x)
.

(iii):

For each
x
, define
\mathsf{P}^{c}(x):=\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{P}(x)
. Since
\mathsf{E}(x)
is an effect and
\mathsf{P}(x)
projects onto the eigenvalue-1 eigenspace of
\mathsf{E}(x)
, it trivially holds that
\psi\in\supp(\mathsf{P}(x))\implies\psi\in\ker(\mathsf{P}^{c}(x))
. Now, given that
\psi\in\supp(\mathsf{P}(x))
implies that
\mathsf{P}(x)\psi=\psi
, and denoting the null vector in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
as
\emptyset
, we have

\displaystyle\emptyset

\displaystyle=(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{P}(x))\psi

\displaystyle=(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x))\psi+\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)\psi

\displaystyle=(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x))\psi

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\neq x}\mathsf{E}(y)\psi.

By positivity of
\mathsf{E}(y)
, the above equation implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{y\neq x}\langle\psi|\mathsf{E}(y)\psi\rangle=\sum_{y\neq x}\langle\sqrt{\mathsf{E}(y)}\psi|\sqrt{\mathsf{E}(y)}\psi\rangle=0,

which can be satisfied only if
\sqrt{\mathsf{E}(y)}\psi=\emptyset\implies\mathsf{E}(y)\psi=\emptyset
for all
y\neq x
. We thus have
\psi\in\supp(\mathsf{P}(x))\implies\psi\in\ker(\mathsf{E}(y))\,\forall\,y\neq x
, and so the support of
\mathsf{P}(x)
must be orthogonal to the support of
\mathsf{E}(y)
for all
y\neq x
. That
\mathsf{P}(x)
and
\mathsf{P}(y)
for
x\neq y
have orthogonal supports follows trivially. Similar arguments hold for
\mathsf{Q}(x)
,
\mathsf{Z}(y)
, and
\mathsf{Q}(y)
.

(iv):

It trivially holds that
\|\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)\|<1
and
\|\mathsf{Q}^{c}(x)\|<1
. We thus have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)^{n}=\mathds{O}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{Q}^{c}(x)^{n}=\mathds{O}
. As stated in (iii), the supports of
\mathsf{P}(x)
and
\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)
are orthogonal, and so it holds that
\mathsf{P}(x)\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)=\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)\mathsf{P}(x)=\mathds{O}
. As such, for all
n\in\mathds{N}
we have
\mathsf{E}(x)^{n}=\mathsf{P}(x)+\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)^{n}
. Similarly,
\mathsf{Z}(x)^{n}=\mathsf{Q}(x)+\mathsf{Q}^{c}(x)^{n}
. By (i), it holds that
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)^{n}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})+\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)^{n}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})=\mathsf{E}(x)
for all
n\in\mathds{N}
, and so it must hold that
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)^{n}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})=\mathds{O}
. Similarly,
\mathsf{E}(x)=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x))+\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Q}^{c}(x)^{n})
for all
n\in\mathds{N}
implies that
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Q}^{c}(x))=\mathds{O}
. The claim follows from Lemma
A.3
.

(v):

First, let us note that by repeatability, it holds that
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Z}(y))={\mathcal{I}}_{y}^{*}(\mathsf{E}(x))=\mathds{O}
for all
x\neq y
. By Lemma
A.3
, it follows that
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Z}(y)A)=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A\mathsf{E}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Z}(y))=\mathds{O}
holds for all
A
and
x\neq y
. We may therefore write

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}}\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x^{\prime})A\mathsf{E}(y)\otimes\mathsf{Z}(y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y}\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{E}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)A\mathsf{E}(y)\otimes\mathsf{Z}(y))

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y}\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x)A\mathsf{P}(y)\otimes\mathsf{Q}(y))=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{R}A\mathsf{R}).

In the final line, we have used the fact that
\mathsf{E}(x)=\mathsf{P}(x)+\mathsf{P}^{c}(x)
and
\mathsf{Z}(x)=\mathsf{Q}(x)+\mathsf{Q}^{c}(x)
, together with (iv).

(vi):

We may write

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(\mathsf{E}(x)A)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{R}(\mathsf{E}(x)A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})\mathsf{R})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x))

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}})-\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x)^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}})={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)).

The first line follows from (v), and the third line follows from the definition
\mathsf{Q}(x)^{\perp}:=\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{Q}(x)
. The final line is obtained by (v) and noting that
(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x)^{\perp})\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x)^{\perp}\mathsf{Q}(x)=\mathds{O}
. The relation
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(A\mathsf{E}(x))={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x))
holding for all
A
trivially follows from above and by observing that
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(\mathsf{E}(x)A^{*})^{*}={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(A\mathsf{E}(x))
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(\mathsf{P}(x)A^{*}\mathsf{P}(x))^{*}={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x))
.

Similarly, we may write

\displaystyle\Lambda^{*}(\mathsf{Z}(x)B)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{R}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)B)\mathsf{R})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x)B\mathsf{Q}(x))

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x)B\mathsf{Q}(x))-\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)^{\perp}\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x)B\mathsf{Q}(x))

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x)B\mathsf{Q}(x))=\Lambda^{*}(\mathsf{Q}(x)B\mathsf{Q}(x)).

(vii):

We may write

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{x}^{*}(A)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{R}(A\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x))\mathsf{R})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Q}(x))

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x))-\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Q}^{c}(x))

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x))={\mathcal{I}}_{x}^{*}(\mathsf{P}(x)A\mathsf{P}(x)).

In the first line we have used (v), in the third line we use
\mathsf{Q}(x)=\mathsf{Z}(x)-\mathsf{Q}^{c}(x)
, and in the final line we use (iv).

∎

Let us highlight one interesting property of repeatable instruments: if
{\mathcal{I}}
is repeatable, then for all input states
\rho
, the output states will be perfectly distinguishable. For the input state
\rho
, we define the normalised post-measurement states as
\rho_{x}:={\mathcal{I}}_{x}(\rho)/\mathrm{tr}[{\mathcal{I}}_{x}(\rho)]
for any
x
satisfying
\mathrm{tr}[{\mathcal{I}}_{x}(\rho)]>0
. By item (vii) of the above proposition, the Schrödinger picture operations of a repeatable instrument satisfy
{\mathcal{I}}_{x}(T)=\mathsf{P}(x){\mathcal{I}}_{x}(T)\mathsf{P}(x)
for all
x
and
T\in{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
, and so
\rho_{x}
will have support only in the eigenvalue-1 eigenspace of
\mathsf{E}(x)
. But by item (iii), such eigenvalue-1 eigenspaces are orthogonal, and so it holds that
\rho_{x}\rho_{y}=\rho_{y}\rho_{x}=\mathds{O}
for all
x\neq y
.

Appendix F
Conservation laws

Recall that by Definition
1
a channel
\Phi
conserves
N
on average if
\Phi^{*}(N)=N
, while by Definition
2

\Phi
fully conserves
N
if
\Phi^{*}(N^{k})=N^{k}
for all
k\in\mathds{N}
. We shall now show that full conservation is in fact equivalent to just the first two moments being conserved, and that is is also equivalent to “invariance” of the unitary group generated by
N
under the action of
\Phi^{*}
, i.e., that
\Phi^{*}(e^{\mathfrak{i}tN})=e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}
for all
t\in\mathds{R}
.

Proposition F.1
.

Let
\Phi:{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})
be a channel, and let
N\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}})
be a self-adjoint operator. The following statements are equivalent:

(i)

\Phi
fully conserves
N
.

(ii)

\Phi^{*}(N^{k})=N^{k}
for
k=1,2
.

(iii)

\Phi^{*}(e^{\mathfrak{i}tN})=e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}
for all
t\in\mathds{R}
.

Proof.

(i)
\implies
(ii): This is trivial.

(ii)
\implies
(i): Assume that
\Phi^{*}(N^{k})=N^{k}
for
k=1,2
. For any
k\geqslant 2
, Corollary
A.1
implies that
\Phi^{*}(N^{k+1})=\Phi^{*}(N^{k}N)=\Phi^{*}(N^{k})N
. The claim follows by induction.

(ii)
\implies
(iii): Define
f(t):=\Phi^{*}(e^{\mathfrak{i}tN})
. Since
N
is bounded,
e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}
is bounded and strongly continuous, and
\Phi^{*}
is a channel, then
f(t)
is infinitely differentiable. Now assume that
\Phi^{*}(N^{k})=N^{k}
for
k=1,2
. It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}f(t)=\mathfrak{i}\Phi^{*}(Ne^{\mathfrak{i}tN})=\mathfrak{i}N\Phi^{*}(e^{itN})=\mathfrak{i}Nf(t),

where the second equality follows from Corollary
A.1
. Indeed, by induction we obtain
(d^{k}/dt^{k})f(t)=\mathfrak{i}^{k}N^{k}f(t)
for all
k\in\mathds{N}
. Since
f(0)=\mathds{1}
, by Taylor expansion around
t=0
we observe that

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(e^{\mathfrak{i}tN})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\left(\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}f(t)\right)\bigg|_{t=0}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{i}^{k}t^{k}}{k!}N^{k}=e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}.

(iii)
\implies
(i): Assume that
\Phi^{*}(e^{\mathfrak{i}tN})=e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}
for all
t\in\mathds{R}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}\Phi^{*}(e^{\mathfrak{i}tN})=\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}\implies\Phi^{*}(N^{k}e^{\mathfrak{i}tN})=N^{k}e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}

holds for all
k\in\mathds{N}
and all
t
. Since
e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}=\mathds{1}
when
t=0
, it follows that
\Phi^{*}(N^{k})=N^{k}
for all
k
.
∎

A property that channels may enjoy is “covariance” under the action of a unitary group, i.e., that
\Phi^{*}(e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}Ae^{-\mathfrak{i}tN})=e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}\Phi^{*}(A)e^{-\mathfrak{i}tN}
holds for all
t\in\mathds{R}
and
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
. While a channel may be coavariant while not invariant—for example,
\Phi^{*}(\cdot)=\mathrm{tr}[\cdot\omega]\mathds{1}
such that
[\omega,N]=\mathds{O}
is covariant but not invariant—we now show that invariance implies covariance.

Corollary F.1
.

Let
\Phi:{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})
be a channel, and let
N\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}})
be a self-adjoint operator. If
\Phi
fully conserves
N
, then for all
t\in\mathds{R}
and
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
it holds that

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}Ae^{-\mathfrak{i}tN})=e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}\Phi^{*}(A)e^{-\mathfrak{i}tN}

Proof.

Let us define
V(t):=e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}
for notational simplicity. By Proposition
F.1
, full conservation of
N
by
\Phi
implies that
\Phi^{*}(V(t))=V(t)
for all
t
. In particular, noting that
V(t)^{*}=V(-t)
, this implies that
\Phi^{*}(V(t)^{*}V(t))-\Phi^{*}(V(t)^{*})\Phi^{*}(V(t))=\Phi^{*}(\mathds{1})-\mathds{1}=\mathds{O}
. As such, by Corollary
A.1
it follows that

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(V(t)AV(-t))=V(t)\Phi^{*}(AV(-t))=V(t)\Phi^{*}(A)V(-t).

∎

We note that the condition
\Phi^{*}(N^{k})=N^{k}
for
k=1,2
was taken as a potential definition of conservation
simpliciter
in Ref.
[
93
]
. However, the authors here conjectured that, in finite dimensions, the condition
\Phi^{*}(N^{2})=N^{2}
may be dropped, and that (in our formulation) both average and full conservation are equivalent. We shall now address this issue: by a simple counter-example, we shall show that average and full conservation are in fact not equivalent for general channels, even in finite dimensions.

Let us consider a system
{\mathcal{H}}\simeq\mathds{C}^{3}
with an orthonormal basis
\{|-1\rangle,|0\rangle,|1\rangle\}
. Now consider
N=\sum_{n}n|n\rangle\langle n|\equiv|1\rangle\langle 1|-|-1\rangle\langle-1|
, and a channel
\Phi^{*}
defined by

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(A)=\langle 1|A|1\rangle|1\rangle\langle 1|+\langle-1|A|-1\rangle|-1\rangle\langle-1|+\langle+|A|+\rangle|0\rangle\langle 0|,

to hold for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
, where we define
|+\rangle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|1\rangle+|-1\rangle)
. It is simple to verify that
\Phi^{*}(N)=N
, that is,
\Phi
conserves
N
on average. However,
\Phi^{*}(N^{2})=\mathds{1}\neq N^{2}
, and so
\Phi
does not fully conserve
N
. Since full conservation is equivalent to invariance, then it follows that
\Phi
is also not invariant. Indeed, we can easily verify that
\Phi
is not covariant either; for example, if we choose
A=|+\rangle\langle+|
, then it holds that

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}|+\rangle\langle+|e^{-\mathfrak{i}tN})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(|1\rangle\langle 1|+|-1\rangle\langle-1|)+\cos(t)^{2}|0\rangle\langle 0|,

\displaystyle e^{\mathfrak{i}tN}\Phi^{*}(|+\rangle\langle+|)e^{-\mathfrak{i}tN}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(|1\rangle\langle 1|+|-1\rangle\langle-1|)+|0\rangle\langle 0|,

which coincide only when
t
is an integer multiple of
\pi
.

While the above discussion shows that average conservation is in general a weaker condition than full conservation, we shall now show that in the special case of unitary channels, average and full conservation are equivalent:

Lemma F.1
.

Let
\Phi(\cdot):=U(\cdot)U^{*}
be a unitary channel, with
U\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
a unitary operator, and let
N\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}})
be a self-adjoint operator. The following statements are equivalent:

(i)

[U,N]=\mathds{O}
.

(ii)

\Phi
conserves
N
on average.

(iii)

\Phi
fully conserves
N
.

Proof.

(i)
\iff
(ii), (i)
\implies
(iii), and (iii)
\implies
(ii) are trivial. To show (iii)
\implies
(i), let us first write

\displaystyle[U,N]^{*}[U,N]=\Phi^{*}(N^{2})+N^{2}-\Phi^{*}(N)N-N\Phi^{*}(N).

If
\Phi
fully conserves
N
, then the right hand side vanishes. But since the left hand side is a positive operator, then it holds that
[U,N]=\mathds{O}
. Finally, we shall show that (ii)
\implies
(iii). Since
\Phi
is unitary, then
\Phi^{*}(A^{*})\Phi^{*}(B)=U^{*}A^{*}UU^{*}BU=U^{*}A^{*}BU=\Phi^{*}(A^{*}B)
holds for all
A,B\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
. If
\Phi^{*}(N)=N
, it follows that
\Phi^{*}(N^{2})=N^{2}
. The claim follows from Proposition
F.1
.
∎

Appendix G
Bounds for measurement error under conservation laws

Here we provide quantitative trade-off relations for measurement error under additive conservation laws, both average and full.

Theorem G.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}:=({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi,{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{Z})
be a measurement scheme for an observable acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, and assume that
{\mathcal{E}}
conserves an additive quantity
N={N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}+\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
on average, where
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
and
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
. Let
\|\epsilon(x)\|
be the error in measuring the effects of the target observable
\mathsf{E}
, as defined in Eq. (
7
). Then for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
it holds that

\displaystyle\|[\mathsf{E}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]-\Lambda^{*}([\mathsf{Z}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}])\|\leqslant 2\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|\|\epsilon(x)\|+2\|\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(N^{2})-\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(N)^{2}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(2\|\epsilon(x)\|+\|\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}\|\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(35)

where
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}
is the channel defined in Eq. (
3
), and
\Lambda
is the conjugate channel to
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
defined in Eq. (
5
).

Proof.

By Eq. (
7
), we have

\displaystyle[\mathsf{E}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]-\Lambda^{*}([\mathsf{Z}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}])=[{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}},\epsilon(x)]+[\Lambda^{*}(\mathsf{Z}(x)),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]-\Lambda^{*}([\mathsf{Z}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}]).

Since
N
is additive, average conservation of
N
by
{\mathcal{E}}
implies that
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(N)=\Gamma_{\xi}(N)={N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}+\mathrm{tr}[{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\xi]\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
. It follows that for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
,
[A,N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}]=[A,\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(N)]
, and so we may write
[\Lambda^{*}(\mathsf{Z}(x)),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]=[\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)),\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(N)]
. Moreover, by additivity of
N
we have
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}([\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x),N])=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes[\mathsf{Z}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}])=\Lambda^{*}([\mathsf{Z}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}])
. We may therefore write

\displaystyle[\mathsf{E}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]-\Lambda^{*}([\mathsf{Z}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}])=[{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}},\epsilon(x)]+[\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)),\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(N)]-\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}([\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x),N]).

(36)

By the sesquilinear mapping
\langle\langle A|B\rangle\rangle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A^{*}B)-\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(A^{*})\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(B)
and Corollary
A.2
we obtain from Eq. (
36
) the bound

\displaystyle\|[\mathsf{E}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]-\Lambda^{*}([\mathsf{Z}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}])\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|[{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}},\epsilon(x)]\|+2\|\langle\langle N|N\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\langle\langle\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)\rangle\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Note that
\|[{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}},\epsilon(x)]\|\leqslant 2\|N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\|\|\epsilon(x)\|
, and that
\langle\langle N|N\rangle\rangle=\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(N^{2})-\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}(N)^{2}
by definition. Given that
\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)
and
\mathsf{E}(x)
are effects and
\Gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}}
is a channel, Lemma
A.2
gives
\|\langle\langle\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{Z}(x)\rangle\rangle\|\leqslant 2\|\epsilon(x)\|+\|\mathsf{E}(x)-\mathsf{E}(x)^{2}\|
. We thus obtain the bound given in Eq. (
35
).
∎

Proposition G.1
.
