\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\xi,P}^{\mathcal{E}}([A,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}})={\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{P}([A,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]).

We thus have
A\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{P})\implies[A,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{P})
, and since
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{P})\subset(P\mathsf{E}P)^{\prime}
, it follows that
[A,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]\in(P\mathsf{E}P)^{\prime}
.
∎

We are now ready to generalise Theorem
L.1
.

Theorem M.1
.

Let
\mathsf{E}:=\{\mathsf{E}(x):x\in{\mathcal{X}}\}
and
\mathsf{F}:=\{\mathsf{F}(y):y\in{\mathcal{Y}}\}
be observables acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}:=({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi,{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{Z})
be a measurement scheme for an
\mathsf{E}
-instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
, and assume that
{\mathcal{E}}
conserves an additive quantity
N={N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}+\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
on average, where
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
and
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
. If
P
is the minimal support projection on
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
, then the following hold:

(i)

\mathsf{F}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
only if
P\mathsf{F}P
commutes with
P\mathsf{E}P
, with
P\Delta{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P:=P{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}({N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})P-P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
, and with

\{[P\mathsf{E}(x)P,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]:x\in{\mathcal{X}}\}
.

(ii)

{\mathcal{I}}
is a measurement of the first kind only if
P\mathsf{E}P
is commutative and commutes with
P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
.

(iii)

{\mathcal{I}}
is repeatable only if
P\mathsf{E}P
is sharp and commutes with
P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
.

Proof.

(i):

By Proposition
M.1
,
P{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})P={\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{P})
, and so
\mathsf{F}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
implies that
P\mathsf{F}P\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{P})
. By Lemma
M.4
, it holds that
P\mathsf{F}P
must commute with
P\mathsf{E}P
, and that
[P\mathsf{F}(y)P,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]={\mathcal{I}}_{P}^{*}([P\mathsf{F}(y)P,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P])
. Given that
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{P})
is a von Neumann algebra, we have
{\mathcal{I}}_{P}^{*}((P\mathsf{F}(y)P)^{2})={\mathcal{I}}_{P}^{*}(P\mathsf{F}(y)P)^{2}=(P\mathsf{F}(y)P)^{2}
. By Corollary
A.1
it follows that
[P\mathsf{F}(y)P,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]=[P\mathsf{F}(y)P,{\mathcal{I}}_{P}^{*}(P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P)]
. By item (i) of Lemma
M.3
, it holds that
{\mathcal{I}}_{P}^{*}(P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P)={\mathcal{I}}_{P}^{*}({N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})=P{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}({N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})P
. Therefore,
P\mathsf{F}P
must commute with
P\Delta{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
. Moreover, by Lemma
M.4
non-disturbance implies that
[P\mathsf{F}(y)P,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]\in(P\mathsf{E}P)^{\prime}
, and since
P\mathsf{F}P
commutes with
P\mathsf{E}P
, this implies that
[P\mathsf{F}(y)P,[P\mathsf{E}(x)P,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]]=\mathds{O}
must hold.

(ii):

If
{\mathcal{I}}
is a measurement of the first kind,
\mathsf{E}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
, it follows from Proposition
M.1
that
P\mathsf{E}P\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{P})
. By Lemma
M.4

P\mathsf{E}P
must be commutative, and since
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{P})
is a von Neumann algebra, then for all spectral projections
R\in{\mathcal{L}}(P{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
of
P\mathsf{E}(x)P
it must hold that
[R,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]
commutes with
P\mathsf{E}P
. But this implies that
[[R,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P],R^{\perp}]=\mathds{O}
, where we define
R^{\perp}:=P-R
. As such, given that
PR=RP=R
, it holds that
[R{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P-P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}R,R^{\perp}]=R{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}R^{\perp}+R^{\perp}{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}R=\mathds{O}
. Multiplying from the left by
R
, we thus have
R{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}R^{\perp}=R{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}(P-R)=\mathds{O}
, and so
R{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P=R{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}R
. Since the right hand side is self-adjoint, and
R{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P=RP{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
, it follows that
[R,P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P]=\mathds{O}
. Since this relation holds for all spectral projections
R
of all effects of
P\mathsf{E}P
, it follows that
P\mathsf{E}P
must commute with
P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
.

(iii):

Commutativity of
P\mathsf{E}P
with
P{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}P
follows from (ii) and the fact that repeatability implies first-kindness. Sharpness of
P\mathsf{E}P
follows from the fact that the fixed points of a repeatable instrument can have support only in the eigenvalue-1 eigenspaces of
\mathsf{E}
, as shown in Proposition
E.1
.

∎

Note that if
P=\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
, implying that
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
contains a faithful state so that
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
is a von Neumann algebra, then the above theorem reduces to
Theorem
L.1
. Interestingly, in the case of qubits such an equivalence will always hold, even if
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
does not contain a faithful state. We demonstrate this by an alternative proof for Proposition 6 in Ref.
[
4
]
.

Corollary M.1
.

If
\dim({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})=2
, then Theorem
M.1
reduces to Theorem
L.1
.

Proof.

Let us consider the minimal support projection
P
on the fixed-point set
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
. As all channels must have at least one fixed state, then when
\dim({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})=2
it holds that, for any instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
, either
P=\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
or
P
is a rank-1 projection. If
P=\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
then
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
contains a faithful state, so that by Lemma
B.1

{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
is a von Neumann algebra, and Theorem
M.1
reduces to Theorem
L.1
. Now assume that
P
is a rank-1 projection, so that for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
it holds that
PAP=\lambda P
, with some
\lambda\in\mathds{C}
. Recall from Lemma
M.1
that
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})={\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*})
. By item (ii) of Lemma
M.2
, for all
A\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
it holds that
A={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*}(A)={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*}(PAP)=\lambda{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*}(P)=\lambda\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
, and so
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
is a trivial von Neumann algebra containing only operators proportional to the identity. In this case, the only non-disturbed observables are trivial, and will clearly commute with all of
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
. In such a case we may simply replace
P
with
\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
in items (i)-(iii) of Theorem
M.1
, so that it reduces to Theorem
L.1
.

∎

M.3
Non-disturbance and distinguishability

Here, we present some novel results regarding the structure of non-disturbing measurements that go beyond those in the preceding sections, indicating an intimate relationship between non-disturbance and distinguishability. These results hold for general instruments, are independent of conservation laws, and do not explicitly depend on the support projection
P
on the fixed-point set of the specific measurement channel
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
. First, let us show that if an instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
does not disturb a non-trivial observable, then there exists a family of distinguishable states that remain distinguishable after a non-selective measurement by
{\mathcal{I}}
.

Proposition M.2

(Non-disturbance implies distinguishability)
.

Consider an instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, and assume that
{\mathcal{I}}
does not disturb a non-trivial observable
\mathsf{F}
. Then there exists a norm-1 observable
\mathsf{G}:=\{\mathsf{G}(z):z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
that is non-disturbed by
{\mathcal{I}}
, so that for every family of states
\{\rho_{z}:z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\}
that are perfectly distinguishable by a
\mathsf{G}
measurement,
\{{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\rho_{z}):z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\}
remain perfectly distinguishable by a
\mathsf{G}
measurement. Moreover, if
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
contains a faithful state, then
\mathsf{G}
can be taken as a sharp observable.

Proof.

Suppose that a non-trivial observable
\mathsf{F}:=\{\mathsf{F}(y):y\in{\mathcal{Y}}\}
is non-disturbed by
{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e,
\mathsf{F}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})={\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*})
. Given that
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})=P{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av}})P
(see Proposition
M.1
), this implies that
P\mathsf{F}(y)P\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})
. That
\mathsf{F}
is non-trivial implies that there must be a
y
for which
P\mathsf{F}(y)P
is not proportional to
P
. If that were the case every
P\mathsf{F}(y)P
could be written as
P\mathsf{F}(y)P=c_{y}P

with some
c_{y}\geqslant 0
, which would imply that
\mathsf{F}(y)={\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av}}(\mathsf{F}(y))={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*}(P\mathsf{F}(y)P)=c_{y}{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*}(P)=c_{y}\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
, and so
\mathsf{F}
would be a trivial observable.
Therefore
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})=P{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av}})P
is a nontrivial von Neumann algebra in
{\mathcal{L}}(P{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
, and there exists a family of projections
\mathsf{R}:=\{\mathsf{R}(z):z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})
satisfying
\mathsf{R}(z)\mathsf{R}(y)=\delta_{z,y}\mathsf{R}(z)
and
\sum_{z}\mathsf{R}(z)=P
. We may consider
\mathsf{R}
as a sharp observable acting in
P{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
.

Using
\mathsf{R}
, we may define a (generally unsharp) observable
\mathsf{G}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}:=\{\mathsf{G}(z)={\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av}}(\mathsf{R}(z)):z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\},

where
\sum_{z}\mathsf{G}(z)={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*}(P)=\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
. Given that
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}\circ{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*}={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*}
(see Lemma
M.1
), it follows that
\mathsf{G}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
, i.e.,
\mathsf{G}
is non-disturbed by
{\mathcal{I}}
. Moreover, since
P\mathsf{G}(z)P={\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P}(\mathsf{R}(z))=\mathsf{R}(z)
holds, then each (non-zero) effect of
\mathsf{G}
has at least one eigenvector with eigenvalue
1
, and so
\|\mathsf{G}(z)\|=1
. Moreover, if
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
contains a faithful state, then
P=\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
, and so we have
\mathsf{G}(z)=P\mathsf{G}(z)P=\mathsf{R}(z)
, implying that
\mathsf{G}\equiv\mathsf{R}
is sharp. Now let us note that the family of states
\{\rho_{z}\}
are perfectly distinguishable given a measurement of
\mathsf{G}
if and only if
\rho_{z}=\mathsf{P}(z)\rho_{z}\mathsf{P}(z)
, where
\mathsf{P}(z)\geqslant\mathsf{R}(z)
projects onto the eigenvalue-1 eigenspace of
\mathsf{G}(z)
. In such a case it trivially holds that
\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{G}(y)\rho_{z}]=\delta_{z,y}
. But Since
\mathsf{G}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
, we also have

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{G}(y){\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\rho_{z})]=\mathrm{tr}[{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}(\mathsf{G}(y))\rho_{z}]=\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{G}(y)\rho_{z}]=\delta_{z,y},

and so
\{{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\rho_{z})\}
continues to be perfectly distinguishable by a
\mathsf{G}
measurement.
∎

In the special case where
{\mathcal{I}}
is a measurement of the first kind, we may strengthen the above result as follows:

Theorem M.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{I}}
be an instrument compatible with a non-trivial observable
\mathsf{E}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. If
{\mathcal{I}}
is a measurement of the first kind, then

\mathsf{E}
is described by a classical post-processing of a norm-1 observable
\mathsf{G}:=\{\mathsf{G}(z):z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\}
with properties given in Proposition
M.2
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}(x)=\sum_{z}p(x|z)\mathsf{G}(z),

(58)

where
\{p(x|z)\}
is a family of non-negative numbers that satisfy
\sum_{x}p(x|z)=1
for each
z
.

Proof.

Assume that the
\mathsf{E}
-instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
is a measurement of the first kind, that is,
\mathsf{E}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})
. It follows that
P\mathsf{E}P\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})
. In fact, we can show that
P\mathsf{E}P\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})\cap{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})^{\prime}
. First, recall from Proposition
M.2
that if
\mathsf{E}
is non-trivial then there exists a family of projections
\mathsf{R}:=\{\mathsf{R}(z):z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})
, satisfying
\mathsf{R}(z)\mathsf{R}(y)=\delta_{z,y}\mathsf{R}(z)
and
\sum_{z}\mathsf{R}(z)=P
. If
P\mathsf{E}P\not\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})\cap{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})^{\prime}
, then
\mathsf{R}
can be chosen so that
[P\mathsf{E}(x)P,\mathsf{R}(z)]\neq\mathds{O}
for some
x
and
z
. But note that
\mathsf{R}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})=P{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}})P
implies that the observable
\{P{\mathcal{I}}_{x}^{*}(\mathsf{R}(z))P,P{\mathcal{I}}_{x}^{*}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{R}(z))P:x\in{\mathcal{X}}\}
is a joint measurement for
P\mathsf{E}P
and the sharp observable
\{\mathsf{R}(z),P-\mathsf{R}(z)\}
. By compatibility, it follows that
[P\mathsf{E}(x)P,\mathsf{R}(z)]=\mathds{O}
must hold for all
x
and
z
, and so
P\mathsf{E}P
must be contained in the Abelian algebra
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})\cap{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})^{\prime}
. Consequently,
\mathsf{R}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})\cap{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av},P})^{\prime}
can be chosen so as to simultaneously diagonalise all
P\mathsf{E}(x)P
, that is, we may write
P\mathsf{E}(x)P=\sum_{z}p(x|z)\mathsf{R}(z)
. Recalling that
\mathsf{E}(x)={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*}(\mathsf{E}(x))={\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{av}}^{*}(P\mathsf{E}(x)P)
, then defining the observable
\mathsf{G}
by
\mathsf{G}(z)={\mathcal{I}}^{*}_{\mathrm{av}}(\mathsf{R}(z))
gives us Eq. (
58
). As in Proposition
M.2
it holds that
\mathsf{G}\subset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*})
,
\mathsf{G}
is a norm-1 observable, if
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}})
contains a faithful state then
\mathsf{G}
is also sharp, and if
\{\rho_{z}\}
are perfectly distinguishable by a
\mathsf{G}
measurement then so are
\{{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(\rho_{z})\}
.
∎

Finally, we present the following implication of the above theorem:

Corollary M.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{I}}
be an instrument compatible with a non-trivial observable
\mathsf{E}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, and assume that
{\mathcal{I}}
is a measurement of the first kind. For any outcome
x
associated with a non-trivial effect
\mathsf{E}(x)
, and for any pair of unit vectors
\psi,\phi\in{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
satisfying
\mathsf{E}(x)\psi=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|\psi
and
(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x))\phi=\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|\phi
, respectively, it holds that
\psi
and
\phi
are orthogonal, and that
F({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\psi}),{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\phi}))=0
, where
F(\rho,\sigma):=\mathrm{tr}[\sqrt{\sqrt{\rho}\sigma\sqrt{\rho}}]
is the fidelity between states
\rho
and
\sigma
.

Proof.

For each outcome
x
, we may coarse-grain
\mathsf{E}
into a binary observable
\{\mathsf{E}(x),\mathsf{E}(\overline{x}):=\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\}
. By Theorem
M.2
, first-kindness implies that
\mathsf{E}(x)=\sum_{z}p(x|z)\mathsf{G}(z)
and
\mathsf{E}(\overline{x})=\sum_{z}p(\overline{x}|z)\mathsf{G}(z)
, with
p(\overline{x}|z):=1-p(x|z)
, where
\{\mathsf{G}(z):z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\}
is a norm-1 observable with properties given in Proposition
M.2
, while
\{p(x|z)\}
is a family of non-negative numbers satisfying
\sum_{x^{\prime}}p(x^{\prime}|z)=1
for each
z
. Now let us define
p_{\max}(x):=\max_{z}\{p(x|z)\}
and
p_{\min}(x):=\min_{z}\{p(x|z)\}
as the maximum and minimum values of the set
\{p(x|z)\}
. We may thus define the sets
Z_{\max}:=\{z\in{\mathcal{Z}}:p(x|z)=p_{\max}(x)\}
and
Z_{\min}:=\{z\in{\mathcal{Z}}:p(x|z)=p_{\min}(x)\}
. Using such sets, we may define
\mathsf{G}(Z_{\max}):=\sum_{z\in Z_{\max}}\mathsf{G}(z)
and
\mathsf{G}(Z_{\min}):=\sum_{z\in Z_{\min}}\mathsf{G}(z)
. Since
\mathsf{G}
is norm-1, then we may also define
\mathsf{P}(Z_{\max}):=\sum_{z\in Z_{\max}}\mathsf{P}(z)
and
\mathsf{P}(Z_{\min}):=\sum_{z\in Z_{\min}}\mathsf{P}(z)
, where
\mathsf{P}(z)
is the projection onto the eigenvalue-1 eigenspace of
\mathsf{G}(z)
. Since
\mathsf{E}(x)
is assumed to be non-trivial, then it must hold that
Z_{\max}\cap Z_{\min}=\emptyset
. If this were not so, it would hold that all
p(x|z)
are the same, in which case
\mathsf{E}(x)\propto\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
. Consequently,
\mathsf{P}(Z_{\max})\mathsf{P}(Z_{\min})=\mathds{O}
.

Now let us note that

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|=\sup_{\|\psi\|=1}\langle\psi|\mathsf{E}(x)\psi\rangle=\sup_{\|\psi\|=1}\sum_{z}p(x|z)\langle\psi|\mathsf{G}(z)\psi\rangle.

We may now show that a unit vector
\psi
satisfies
\mathsf{E}(x)\psi=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|\psi
if and only if
\mathsf{P}(Z_{\max})\psi=\psi
. Let us first prove the only if statement. For any unit vector
\psi
, it holds that
\langle\psi|\mathsf{E}(x)\psi\rangle\leqslant p_{\max}(x)
, which follows from the fact that
p(x|z)
are positive numbers and that
\{\langle\psi|\mathsf{G}(z)\psi\rangle\}
is a probability distribution, with the upper bound being saturated when
\langle\psi|\mathsf{G}(Z_{\max})\psi\rangle=1
. But this in turn is satisfied only if
\langle\psi|\mathsf{P}(Z_{\max})\psi\rangle=1
, in which case
\mathsf{P}(Z_{\max})\psi=\psi
. As such, it follows that
\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|=p_{\max}(x)
, and the unit vector
\psi
satisfies
\mathsf{E}(x)\psi=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|\psi
only if
\mathsf{P}(Z_{\max})\psi=\psi
. The if statement is trivial.

By similar arguments as above, we may show that
\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|=\|\mathsf{E}(\overline{x})\|=p_{\max}(\overline{x})=1-p_{\min}(x)
, and that the unit vector
\phi
satisfies
(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x))\phi=\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|\phi
if and only if
\mathsf{P}(Z_{\min})\phi=\phi
. Since
\mathsf{E}(x)
is non-trivial, then as argued above
\psi
and
\phi
are orthogonal, and perfectly distinguishable by a
\mathsf{G}
measurement. By Proposition
M.2
it holds that
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\psi})
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\phi})
are also perfectly distinguishable by a
\mathsf{G}
measurement, that is,
F({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\psi}),{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\phi}))=0
.

∎

M.4
Measurements of the first kind, distinguishability, and the Wigner-Araki-Yanase theorem

We shall now use the results in the preceding section to obtain quantitative bounds for first-kind measurements in the presence of a conservation law, that complement our generalisation of the WAY theorem given in Theorem
3.3
. To this end, let us first provide a generalisation of Theorem 2 in Ref.
[
27
]
, which we shall use in the sequel:

Lemma M.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}:=({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi,{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{Z})
be a measurement scheme for an
\mathsf{E}
-instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, and assume that
{\mathcal{E}}
conserves an additive quantity
N={N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}+\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
on average, where
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
and
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
. For any pair of orthogonal unit vectors
\psi,\phi\in{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, the following will hold:

\displaystyle|\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle|\leqslant\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\|F({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\psi}),{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\phi}))+\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|F(\Lambda(P_{\psi}),\Lambda(P_{\phi})),

(59)

where
\Lambda
is the conjugate channel to
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
defined in Eq. (
5
), and
F(\rho,\sigma)
is the fidelity between states
\rho
and
\sigma
.

Proof.

Let us consider the augmented Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
so that
\xi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
admits the purification
\xi=\mathrm{tr}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}}[P_{\varphi}]
, with the unit vector
\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
. Moreover, if
{\mathcal{K}}
is sufficiently large, then by Stinespring’s dilation theorem the channel
{\mathcal{E}}^{*}
can be expressed as
{\mathcal{E}}^{*}(A)=V^{*}(A\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V
for all
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
, where
V:{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\to{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
is an isometry. By additivity of
N
, and orthogonality of
\psi,\phi
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\psi\otimes\varphi|N\phi\otimes\varphi\rangle=\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle.

On the other hand, average conservation of
N
by
{\mathcal{E}}
implies that

\displaystyle N={\mathcal{E}}^{*}(N)=V^{*}({N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V+V^{*}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V.

We therefore have

\displaystyle|\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle|\leqslant|\langle\psi\otimes\varphi|V^{*}({N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V\phi\otimes\varphi\rangle|+|\langle\psi\otimes\varphi|V^{*}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V\phi\otimes\varphi\rangle|.

(60)

For any observable
\mathsf{G}:=\{\mathsf{G}(z)\}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}
, we may write

\displaystyle|\langle\psi\otimes\varphi|V^{*}({N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V\phi\otimes\varphi\rangle|

\displaystyle\qquad=|\sum_{z}\langle\psi\otimes\varphi|V^{*}({N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes\mathsf{G}(z)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V\phi\otimes\varphi\rangle|

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|\sum_{z}|\langle\psi\otimes\varphi|V^{*}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{G}(z)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V\phi\otimes\varphi\rangle|

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|\sum_{z}|\langle\psi\otimes\varphi|V^{*}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{G}(z)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V\psi\otimes\varphi\rangle|^{\frac{1}{2}}|\langle\phi\otimes\varphi|V^{*}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{G}(z)\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V\phi\otimes\varphi\rangle|^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|\sum_{z}\mathrm{tr}[\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{G}(z){\mathcal{E}}(P_{\psi}\otimes\xi)]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathsf{G}(z){\mathcal{E}}(P_{\phi}\otimes\xi)]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|\sum_{z}\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{G}(z)\Lambda(P_{\psi})]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{G}(z)\Lambda(P_{\phi})]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

In the third line we have used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, in the fourth line we used Stinespring’s dilation theorem together with the fact that
\varphi
is a purification of
\xi
, and in the final line we use the definitions of the partial trace and the conjugate channel
\Lambda
. Now, note that the fidelity satisfies
F(\rho,\sigma)=\min_{\mathsf{G}}\sum_{z}\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{G}(z)\rho]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{G}(z)\sigma]^{\frac{1}{2}}

[
95
,
96
]
. Therefore, choosing
\mathsf{G}
so as to obtain the fidelity, we have

\displaystyle|\langle\psi\otimes\varphi|V^{*}({N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V\phi\otimes\varphi\rangle|\leqslant\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|F(\Lambda(P_{\psi}),\Lambda(P_{\phi})).

Using similar steps, we may also write

\displaystyle|\langle\psi\otimes\varphi|V^{*}(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{K}}}})V\phi\otimes\varphi\rangle|\leqslant\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\|F({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\psi}),{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\phi})).

By Eq. (
60
), we thus obtain Eq. (
59
).
∎

We are now ready to prove our main result in this section:

Theorem M.3
.

Consider a measurement scheme
{\mathcal{M}}:=({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi,{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{Z})
for a nontrivial observable
\mathsf{E}
with the instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
. Assume that
{\mathcal{I}}
is a measurement of the first kind, and that
{\mathcal{E}}
conserves an additive quantity
N=N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}+\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}
on average, where
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
and
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
.
For each outcome
x
associated with a non-trivial effect
\mathsf{E}(x)
, let
\mathcal{K}_{\max}(x)
and
\mathcal{K}_{\min}(x)
be subspaces of
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\max}(x):=\{\psi\in{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}:\ \mathsf{E}(x)\psi=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|\psi\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\min}(x):=\{\phi\in{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}:\ (\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x))\phi=\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|\phi\}.

\mathcal{K}_{\max}(x)
and
\mathcal{K}_{\min}(x)
are orthogonal, and for all unit vectors
\psi\in\mathcal{K}_{\max}(x)
and
\phi\in\mathcal{K}_{\min}(x)
, it holds that

\displaystyle|\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle|\leqslant\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|\left(\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|^{\frac{1}{2}}(1-\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(1-\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

(61)

Proof.

For each outcome
x
associated with a non-trivial effect
\mathsf{E}(x)
, we may coarse-grain
\mathsf{E}
into a binary observable
\{\mathsf{E}(x),\mathsf{E}(\overline{x}):=\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\}
. By Corollary
M.2
, it holds that for any unit vectors
\psi\in\mathcal{K}_{\max}(x)
and
\phi\in\mathcal{K}_{\min}(x)
,
\psi
and
\phi
are orthogonal—implying that
\mathcal{K}_{\max}(x)
and
\mathcal{K}_{\min}(x)
are orthogonal subspaces—and
F({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\psi}),{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}(P_{\phi}))=0
. As such, given the average conservation of
N
by the interaction channel
{\mathcal{E}}
, Lemma
M.5
implies that the following inequality must hold:

\displaystyle|\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|F\left(\Lambda(P_{\psi}),\Lambda(P_{\phi})\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|\sum_{a=x,\overline{x}}\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{Z}(a)\Lambda(P_{\psi})]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{Z}(a)\Lambda(P_{\phi})]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|\sum_{a=x,\overline{x}}\langle\psi|\mathsf{E}(a)\psi\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle\phi|\mathsf{E}(a)\phi\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\|\left(\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|^{\frac{1}{2}}(1-\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(1-\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

The second line uses the fact that for any states
\rho,\sigma
, it holds that
F(\rho,\sigma)\leqslant\sum_{a}\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{F}(a)\rho]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{F}(a)\sigma]^{\frac{1}{2}}
for any observable
\mathsf{F}

[
95
,
96
]
. The third line uses the fact that
\Lambda
is the conjugate channel to
{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{X}}
defined in Eq. (
5
), and so it holds that
\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{Z}(a)\Lambda(\rho)]=\mathrm{tr}[\mathsf{E}(a)\rho]
for all
\rho
and
a=x,\overline{x}
. To see how the final line is obtained, note that we have
\langle\psi|\mathsf{E}(x)\psi\rangle=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|
and
\langle\phi|(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x))\phi\rangle=\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|
by construction. For the first term, i.e.,
a=x
, we obtain
\langle\psi|\mathsf{E}(a)\psi\rangle=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|
and
\langle\phi|\mathsf{E}(a)\phi\rangle=\langle\phi|(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)))\phi\rangle=1-\langle\phi|(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x))\phi\rangle=1-\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|
. The second term for
a=\overline{x}
is obtained in a similar manner.

∎

Let us note that if
\mathsf{E}
commutes with
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, then Theorem
M.3
imposes no restrictions on first-kindness. To see this, let us note that for any (possibly trivial) effect
\mathsf{E}(x)
, and for any unit vectors
\psi,\phi
satisfying
\mathsf{E}(x)\psi=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|\psi
and
(\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x))\phi=\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|\phi
, it holds that
\langle\psi|\mathsf{E}(x){N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle
and
\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\mathsf{E}(x)\phi\rangle=(1-\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|)\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle
. It follows that if
[\mathsf{E}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]=\mathds{O}
then either (i)
\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle=0
, or (ii)
\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|+\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|=1
. Condition (i) implies that the lower bound of Eq. (
61
) vanishes, and so no constraint is imposed. On the other hand, condition (ii) implies that
\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|+\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|=1+(p_{\max}(x)-p_{\min}(x))=1
, where we recall from Corollary
M.2
that
p_{\max}(x)=\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|
and
p_{\min}(x)=1-\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|
are the largest and smallest values from the set
\{p(x|z)\}
given by Theorem
M.2
. Such equality is satisfied if and only if
p_{\max}(x)=p_{\min}(x)=\lambda
, in which case by Eq. (
58
) it follows that
\mathsf{E}(x)=\lambda\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}
is a trivial effect. But Eq. (
61
) applies only to non-trivial effects, and so no constraints are imposed in such a case.

Let us now show that the first-kindness statement of Theorem
3.3
may be recovered directly from Eq. (
61
).

Corollary M.3
.

Consider a measurement scheme
{\mathcal{M}}:=({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}},\xi,{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{Z})
for an
\mathsf{E}
-instrument
{\mathcal{I}}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}
, and assume that
{\mathcal{E}}
conserves an additive quantity
N=N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}+\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}\otimes N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}
on average, where
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}})
and
{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}}\in{\mathcal{L}_{s}}({\mathcal{H}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{A}}}}})
. If
{\mathcal{I}}
is a measurement of the first kind, then for all effects
\mathsf{E}(x)
that have both eigenvalue 1 and 0,

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}[\mathsf{E}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]\mathsf{P}=[\mathsf{E}(x),\mathsf{P}{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\mathsf{P}]=\mathds{O},

where
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{P}_{0}(x)+\mathsf{P}_{1}(x)
, with
\mathsf{P}_{0}(x)
and
\mathsf{P}_{1}(x)
orthogonal projections onto the eigenvalue-0 and eigenvalue-1 eigenspaces of
\mathsf{E}(x)
, respectively.

Proof.

It is trivial to verify that
\supp(\mathsf{P}_{1}(x))\equiv{\mathcal{K}}_{\max}(x)
and
\supp(\mathsf{P}_{0}(x))\equiv{\mathcal{K}}_{\min}(x)
as defined in Theorem
M.3
. Any
\varphi\in\supp(\mathsf{P})
may be written as
\varphi=\alpha\psi+\beta\phi
, where
\psi\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\max}(x)
and
\phi\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\min}(x)
are unit vectors and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathds{C}
.
Since
\|\mathsf{E}(x)\|=\|\mathds{1}_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}-\mathsf{E}(x)\|=1
for any effect that has both eigenvalue 1 and 0, then by Theorem
M.3
it follows that
\langle\varphi|\mathsf{P}_{1}(x){N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\mathsf{P}_{0}(x)\varphi\rangle=\alpha^{*}\beta\langle\psi|{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\phi\rangle=0
for all
\varphi\in\supp(\mathsf{P})
. It follows that
\mathsf{P}_{1}(x){N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\mathsf{P}_{0}(x)=\mathsf{P}_{1}(x){N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}(\mathsf{P}-\mathsf{P}_{1}(x))=\mathds{O}
, which implies that
\mathsf{P}_{1}(x){N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{P}_{1}(x){N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\mathsf{P}_{1}(x)
. Since the right hand side is self-adjoint, and
\mathsf{P}_{1}(x)=\mathsf{P}_{1}(x)\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{P}\mathsf{P}_{1}(x)
, it follows that
[\mathsf{P}_{1}(x),\mathsf{P}{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\mathsf{P}]=\mathds{O}
. But since
\mathsf{P}_{1}(x)=\mathsf{E}(x)\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{P}\mathsf{E}(x)
, we have
[\mathsf{E}(x),\mathsf{P}{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\mathsf{P}]=\mathsf{P}[\mathsf{E}(x),{N_{\!{\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}]\mathsf{P}=\mathds{O}
. This completes the proof.
∎
